Neurovascular Atlas · Content audit · 18 October 2026

Description corrections for v0.9.121

All 1,295 populated descriptions in v0.9.120 were reviewed for relevance to the named structure, copied parent or sibling text, anatomical overstatement and displayed link labels. The list below was prepared before the changes were applied. It contains the exact replacement text for each affected entry. Bilateral entries are listed separately.

361Artery entries revised
57Vein entries revised
193Brain entries revised
55Bone entries revised
666 revisions. Most are relevance or clarity improvements, not claims that the previous anatomy was wholly wrong.

No geometry, structure names, hierarchy, relationships, arterial segment boundaries, drainage targets or DSA behaviour is changed. Normal variation is retained in the wording. Brain parcels and unresolved regional landmarks remain atlas approximations.

Main findings

  • MCA superior and inferior divisions now describe their own M2 insular / M3 opercular courses and territories. Cortical branches identify their own supply rather than repeating the whole MCA overview.
  • Targeted anatomical corrections include usual medial palpebral origin, central retinal origin and inner retinal supply, posterior superior alveolar canals, the superior orbital fissure artery route, ICA sympathetic origin and NYU paraophthalmic boundaries, external jugular formation and vertebral dural entry.
  • Potential anastomoses name their own catalogue endpoints. This is a description of the selected reference connection, not a new claim of normal conspicuous patency or a verification of its physical geometry.
  • 193 brain entries now state the location and relationships of the named anatomy. Combined or ambiguous source-atlas labels are explicitly qualified.
  • 55 bony landmark entries now describe their location, communications and contents.
  • Displayed venous drainage links no longer contain authoring debris such as “High | New”. Their destinations are retained. The two contralateral pharyngeal links are intentional and are retained.

Scope limits: this is a semantic and anatomical description audit, not a new geometry acceptance review. In particular, the usual ophthalmic origin of medial palpebral arteries is corrected in prose without moving the existing angular-linked reference meshes or changing their catalogue parents. Separate anatomical names, origins or geometry may still require review. The 166 rendered structures with empty descriptions are inventoried below and were not filled in this audit.

Problem list and implemented corrections

StructureProblemReplacement description
1. Angular artery rightartery.eca.angular.rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Incorrectly makes the angular artery the usual source of the medial palpebral arteries.

Previous description

Angular artery: if present, this is the terminal branch of the facial artery. It may also originate from the ophthalmic or infraorbital arteries. The vessel courses in the nasojugal fold giving off the medial palpebral artery supplying the eyelids, and anastomoses with the lateral palpebral artery (lacrimal artery) to form the palpebral arcade. There are anastomoses with the ophthalmic, lateral nasal, and infraorbital arteries in addition to its counterpart across the midline.

The angular artery is a variable terminal continuation of the facial artery near the medial canthus. It supplies the adjacent nose and eyelids and communicates with dorsal nasal and other ophthalmic branches. Angular branches contribute to eyelid circulation, but the usual medial palpebral arteries arise from the ophthalmic circulation.

Cong et al. Topographic Anatomy of the Inferior Medial Palpebral Artery
2. Angular artery leftartery.eca.angular.right.leftAnatomical correction / qualification

3. Posterior deep temporal artery rightartery.eca.middle_temporal.rightRelevance and clarity

Ambiguous temporal-branch terminology; avoid an unsupported fixed origin.

Previous description

Posterior deep temporal artery: this arises at the level of the zygoma and is directed posteroinferiorly. Rarely it originates from the IMA or from a common trunk with the zygomatico-orbital arteries. It supplies the temporalis muscle and anastomoses with the middle and anterior temporal arteries.

This temporal muscular branch supplies the posterior temporalis and communicates with neighbouring temporal arteries. Deep temporal branches and branches of the superficial temporal artery form a variable anastomotic network.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

5. Mental artery rightartery.eca.mental.rightRelevance and clarity

Mostly describes the inferior alveolar bifurcation rather than this terminal branch.

Previous description

The inferior alveolar artery divides into incisor and mental terminal branches opposite the first premolar tooth. The mental branch supplies the lower lip and chin.

The mental artery is a terminal branch of the inferior alveolar artery. It leaves the mandible through the mental foramen and supplies the chin and lower lip, communicating with nearby facial and submental branches.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

7. Incisive artery rightartery.eca.incisive.rightRelevance and clarity

Parent bifurcation text does not describe the selected intraosseous branch.

Previous description

The inferior alveolar artery divides into incisor and mental terminal branches opposite the first premolar tooth. The incisor branch supplies the pulp of the teeth.

The incisive artery continues anteriorly within the mandible from the inferior alveolar arterial system to supply the anterior mandibular teeth and adjacent bone.

9. Mylohyoid artery rightartery.eca.mylohyoid.rightRelevance and clarity

Generic extramandibular branches replace the named mylohyoid course.

Previous description

Extramandibular branches of the inferior alveolar artery supply the pterygoid and mylohyoid muscles and the lingual nerve.

The mylohyoid branch usually leaves the inferior alveolar artery before the mandibular foramen and accompanies the mylohyoid nerve along the medial mandible. It supplies mylohyoid and adjacent tissues and communicates with submental and sublingual branches.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

11. Posterior superior alveolar artery rightartery.eca.maxillary.posterior_superior_alveolar.rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Incorrect extension towards the incisive foramen; the named posterior alveolar territory was obscured.

Previous description

Origin: this artery takes its origin from the proximal part of the third maxillary segment, just inside the pterygopalatine fossa. It often shares a common trunk with the infraorbital artery.

Course: anteroinferiorly on the lateral surface of the maxilla, giving off osseous branches, supplying the mucosa of the maxillary sinus and buccal cavity, and buccinator. The vessel then enters the superior alveolar canal in the maxilla running towards the incisor foramen.

Anastomoses: infraorbital, transverse facial, and descending palatine arteries.

The posterior superior alveolar artery usually arises from the maxillary artery near the pterygopalatine fossa. Branches enter posterior superior alveolar foramina and canals to supply the posterior maxillary teeth, adjacent gingiva and maxillary sinus. Its alveolar anastomoses include infraorbital arterial branches.

Anatomy of the Posterior Superior Alveolar Artery: a CBCT study

13. Posterior septal artery rightartery.eca.posterior_septal.rightRelevance and clarity

Long discussion of a five-vessel anterior plexus replaces the posterior septal course.

Previous description

Posterior septal (or medial sphenopalatine) artery: this vessel is medially directed towards the superior turbinate and gives off a branch to the sphenoid ostium. It divides into superior and inferior branches. The superior branch supplies the superior turbinate before coursing medially to the nasal septum where it curves anteriorly. It anastomoses with branches of the anterior ethmoidal artery and descending palatine and contributes to Kiesselbach’s plexus. This plexus is located on the anterior inferior quadrant of the nasal septum (‘Little’s area’) and is a common site for epistaxis. It is supplied by five vessels: the sphenopalatine, superior labial, greater palatine, anterior ethmoidal, and posterior ethmoidal arteries.

The posterior septal branch of the sphenopalatine artery reaches the nasal septum and runs anteriorly within its mucosa. It supplies the posterior septum and communicates with ethmoidal, greater palatine and superior labial territories.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

15. MMA frontal artery rightartery.eca.maxillary.mma.frontal.rightRelevance and clarity

Orbital anastomoses dominate a description of the frontal meningeal division.

Previous description

Anterior convexity branches: these originate from the temporal, pterional, and coronal segments and course medially along the lesser wing of the sphenoid bone. There are important anastomoses with the ophthalmic artery. The MMA may be the sole supply of the orbit (the ‘meningo-ophthalmic artery’) or supply the lacrimal gland and lateral orbit via a recurrent branch traversing the foramen of Hyrtl (found in the spheno-orbital foramen, in the orbital roof). This latter branch usually also has ophthalmic artery anastomoses. Anterior branches from the coronal segment anastomose with the anterior ethmoidal branch of the ophthalmic artery.

The frontal division of the middle meningeal artery ascends over the sphenoidal and frontal convexity towards the coronal region, supplying the anterior supratentorial dura. It communicates with neighbouring meningeal territories and may give orbital or falcine connections.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

17. MMA orbital artery rightartery.eca.mma_orbital.rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Conflates a local orbital branch with replacement of the entire ophthalmic supply.

Previous description

The MMA may be the sole supply of the orbit (the meningo-ophthalmic artery) or supply the lacrimal gland and lateral orbit via a recurrent branch traversing the foramen of Hyrtl. This latter branch usually also has ophthalmic artery anastomoses.

An orbital branch of the middle meningeal artery can reach the lacrimal or orbital circulation through a cranio-orbital canal or connections near the superior orbital fissure. A meningolacrimal contribution and a meningo-ophthalmic origin are distinct patterns; neither is obligatory.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

19. Central retinal rightartery.anterior.central_retinal_rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Incorrectly fixes the origin within the optic canal and fails to distinguish inner retinal supply.

Previous description

The central retinal artery: this, the most important branch, arises within the optic canal and travels alongside the optic nerve to supply the retina. It is a terminal branch without anastomoses and occlusion may result in permanent and severe visual loss.

The central retinal artery usually arises from the ophthalmic artery within the posterior orbit, with variation in its origin and branching order. It pierces the optic nerve sheath and travels within the nerve to supply the inner retina. It behaves as a functional end artery; the outer retina is mainly supplied by the choroidal circulation.

The morphometric analysis of the central retinal artery
20. Central retinal leftartery.anterior.central_retinal_leftAnatomical correction / qualification

21. Posterior communicating rightartery.anterior.posterior_communicating_rightRelevance and clarity

Embryology is repeated and the actual named connection is not stated first.

Previous description

The PCOM insertion defines the PCA P1-P2 segments. Embryonically, the PCOM represents the caudal division of the ICA (contrasting with the adult description of the ICA division into the middle cerebral artery (MCA) and anterior cerebral artery (ACA)).

A ‘fetal type’ PCOM, seen in approximately 25%, means the vessel is larger than the P1, or that it supplies most of the flow to the PCA territory. Complete absence of the PCOM is very rare; however, a very hypoplastic vessel may not be appreciated angiographically.

Branches: the PCOM gives off numerous small perforators to the ventral thalamus. These may be present even if the vessel is hypoplastic.

In embryonic development the PCOM is the caudal division of the ICA. As the vertebrobasilar system develops and gives rise to the PCA, the PCOM regresses to a variable degree.

The posterior communicating artery connects the supraclinoid internal carotid artery with the posterior cerebral artery at the P1-P2 junction. Its calibre varies from hypoplastic to a dominant fetal configuration. Perforators contribute to the hypothalamic and anterior thalamic circulation.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

23. Inferior hypophyseal rightartery.anterior.inferior_hypophyseal_rightRelevance and clarity

Embryological discussion of other clival vessels dominates this hypophyseal branch.

Previous description

The posterior inferior hypophyseal artery: anastomoses with its contralateral counterpart and supplies the posterior (and sometimes part of the anterior) lobe of the pituitary. It gives off an inferior clival artery that anastomoses with clival branches of the hypoglossal artery. The posterior inferior hypophyseal and medial clival arteries are remnants of the primitive IMA which can persist and enlarge in agenesis of the cervical ICA, so reconstituting the intracranial carotid circulation.

The inferior hypophyseal artery, commonly a meningohypophyseal branch, supplies mainly the posterior pituitary and communicates with its opposite-side counterpart. Small associated branches may supply adjacent sellar and clival dura.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

25. ACA pericallosal rightartery.anterior.aca_pericallosal_rightRelevance and clarity

Segment-by-segment parent narrative obscures the named distal arterial axis.

Previous description

The A2 segment arises distal to the ACOM and extends to the junction of the rostrum and genu of the corpus callosum.

The A3 segment extends around the genu of the corpus callosum.

Segments inclusive of and distal to the A3 are generally called the pericallosal artery, although sometimes the A2 segment is also referred to as such. Small perforating branches supply the corpus callosum and the callosal arteries, and these traverse the corpus callosum to supply the septum, anterior pillars of the fornix, and anterior commissure. Distally, there are anastomoses with the posterior pericallosal branch of the PCA via perisplenial branches.

The A4 segment originates at the body of the corpus callosum and the A5 segment is the continuation of the vessel posterior to the coronal suture.

The pericallosal artery is the distal continuation of the anterior cerebral artery along the corpus callosum. It curves around the genu and continues in the pericallosal cistern, giving callosal and medial hemispheric branches. The precise use of the term and the origins of its cortical branches vary.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

27. Callosomarginal rightartery.anterior.callosomarginal_rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Overly fixed A3 origin and branch count.

Previous description

The callosomarginal artery is the major branch of the A3 segment and runs parallel to the vessel inside the cingulate sulcus. It may be absent, with its branches arising directly from the pericallosal artery. It gives off four arteries that supply the medial frontal lobe: the anterior, middle, and posterior internal frontal arteries, and the paracentral artery.

The callosomarginal artery is a variable major branch of the distal anterior cerebral artery. It runs posteriorly in or near the cingulate sulcus and supplies medial frontal and paracentral territories. It may be absent, and its origin is not restricted to one ACA segment.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

29. Anterior internal frontal rightartery.anterior.anterior_internal_frontal_rightRelevance and clarity

Parent branch list replaces the selected cortical territory.

Previous description

The anterior internal frontal artery is one of four branches of the callosomarginal artery supplying the medial frontal lobe. The callosomarginal artery may be absent, with its branches arising directly from the pericallosal artery.

The anterior internal frontal artery supplies the anterior medial frontal cortex. It may arise from the callosomarginal or pericallosal arterial system, with variable overlap with frontopolar and other internal frontal branches.

39. MCA superior division rightartery.anterior.mca_superior_division_rightRelevance and clarity

Identical division-wide description, dominated by the parent MCA bifurcation rather than this M2/M3 division.

Previous description

The artery usually divides into two trunks, although a single trunk, trifurcation, or quadrifurcation are all possible. Superior and inferior divisions are usually referred to. It is slightly more common for the inferior division to be dominant, covering the temporal and parietal lobes while the superior division tends to supply the frontal lobe. With superior dominance, its territory may extend to the angular gyrus and temporo-occipital regions.

The branches angulate superiorly and course in the Sylvian fissure lateral to the insular cortex.

The superior division of the middle cerebral artery courses over the insula as an M2 trunk, then passes through the operculum as M3 branches to reach the cortex. It predominantly supplies the lateral frontal and perirolandic territories. Its branching pattern and territorial extent vary.

Microsurgical Anatomy of Middle Cerebral Artery, cadaveric study
40. MCA superior division leftartery.anterior.mca_superior_division_leftRelevance and clarity

Identical division-wide description, dominated by the parent MCA bifurcation rather than this M2/M3 division.

Previous description

The artery usually divides into two trunks, although a single trunk, trifurcation, or quadrifurcation are all possible. Superior and inferior divisions are usually referred to. It is slightly more common for the inferior division to be dominant, covering the temporal and parietal lobes while the superior division tends to supply the frontal lobe. With superior dominance, its territory may extend to the angular gyrus and temporo-occipital regions.

The branches angulate superiorly and course in the Sylvian fissure lateral to the insular cortex.

The superior division of the middle cerebral artery courses over the insula as an M2 trunk, then passes through the operculum as M3 branches to reach the cortex. It predominantly supplies the lateral frontal and perirolandic territories. Its branching pattern and territorial extent vary.

43. MCA orbitofrontal rightartery.anterior.mca_orbitofrontal_rightRelevance and clarity

Generic M4 discussion replaces the orbitofrontal branch anatomy.

Previous description

The orbitofrontal artery is one of the frontal branches of the MCA. The cortical branches are named after the regions they supply.

The M4 (cortical) segments commence at the opercular turn and run on the surface of the cerebral convexity. The M4 cortical branches supply the cerebral convexity. The MCA as a whole also supplies the insular and opercular cortex through earlier branches, associated with the M2 insular and M3 opercular portions.

The MCA orbitofrontal artery supplies the lateral orbital surface of the frontal lobe. It may arise as an early MCA branch or from the superior division, with variable overlap with ACA orbitofrontal supply.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

45. Lateral lenticulostriate 1 rightartery.anterior.lateral_lenticulostriate_1_rightRelevance and clarity

Unresolved phrase "this segment" and mixed medial/lateral perforator territories.

Previous description

Lenticulostriate arteries (between 1 and 20, average 10) arise from the superior surface of this segment and perforate the anterior perforated substance. The medial group supplies the globus pallidus and lentiform nucleus. The lateral group supplies the anterior commissure, internal capsule, and upper head and body of the caudate nucleus, putamen, globus pallidus, and substantia innominata. They have an S-shape configuration on the right, and reverse S-shape on the left. The medial group may also arise from the A1 and the latter group may arise from the M2 segments.

A representative lateral lenticulostriate artery arises from the MCA, most often its M1 segment, and enters the anterior perforated substance. These perforators supply variable portions of the striatum, pallidum and internal capsule. The numbered branches are illustrative and do not imply a fixed number or identical territories.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

47. Medial lenticulostriate rightartery.anterior.medial_lenticulostriate_rightRelevance and clarity

Mixes medial striate supply with the broader hypothalamic and optic ACA perforator groups.

Previous description

Medial lenticulostriate arteries: superior branches supply the anterior basal ganglia, anterior commissure, and anterior limb of the internal capsule. Medial branches supply the anterior aspect of the lateral wall of the third ventricle, hypothalamus, and septum pellucidum. Inferior branches infiltrate the optic nerves and chiasm. They vary in size and number.

The medial lenticulostriate arteries are small ACA perforators supplying the anterior basal ganglia and adjacent anterior internal capsule. Their number, origin and overlap with the recurrent artery of Heubner and other ACA perforators vary.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

49. Vertebral rightartery.posterior.vertebral_rightRelevance and clarity

Only states that four segments exist.

Previous description

The vertebral artery can be divided into four segments.

The vertebral artery usually arises from the subclavian artery, ascends through the cervical transverse foramina and enters the cranial cavity near the foramen magnum. The paired intradural vertebral arteries join to form the basilar artery. Its conventional segments are V1 preforaminal, V2 foraminal, V3 atlantal and V4 intradural.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

51. Thalamoperforator 1 rightartery.posterior.thalamoperforator_1_rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Broad list overstates a fixed shared territory for every numbered perforator.

Previous description

Posterior thalamoperforators (up to eight): these enter the posterior perforated substance, the interpeduncular cistern, and medial cerebral peduncles, and supply the posterior part of the thalamus, posterior part of the optic chiasm and tracts, posterior limb of the internal capsule, hypothalamus, subthalamus, substantia nigra, red nucleus, oculomotor and trochlear nuclei, rostral mesencephalon, and cerebral peduncles. The peduncular supply includes the corticospinal and corticobulbar tracts.

A representative posterior thalamoperforating artery arises from the proximal posterior cerebral artery and enters the posterior perforated substance. This variable perforator group supplies the paramedian thalamus and parts of the rostral midbrain. The numbered examples do not represent fixed individual territories.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

73. Posterior spinal rightartery.posterior.posterior_spinal_rightRelevance and clarity

Irrelevant comparison with MCA perforators and a fixed normal diameter distract from the spinal artery.

Previous description

The PSAs are positioned off midline, and hence identifiable on carefully performed true AP spinal angiography. They are sometimes visible arising from the intradural vertebral arteries or posterior inferior cerebellar arteries, particularly in the presence of cervical AVMs. In the non-pathological state, they are small and measure <0.5 mm.

The PSAs and contributing radiculopial arteries create a network to supply the circumference of the periphery of the cord in a centripetal manner. In this instance, the network is analogous to the M2 perforators as they pass into the insular cortex from the Sylvian fissure.

The PSAs principally supply the posterior third of the cord, including the dorsal columns, dorsal grey matter, and dorsal aspects of the lateral columns.

The paired posterior spinal arteries descend on the dorsolateral spinal cord and usually arise cranially from the vertebral arteries or PICAs. Together with radiculopial and pial contributions, they supply the posterior cord, especially the dorsal columns and posterior grey matter.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

A masseteric branch of the facial artery supplies the adjacent masseter and communicates with masseteric branches of the maxillary and transverse facial arteries.

107. Artery of superior orbital fissure rightartery.amendment.artery_of_superior_orbital_fissure.rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Anastomoses: anteromedial branch of the ILT and recurrent meningeal branch of the lacrimal branch of the ophthalmic artery.

The artery of the superior orbital fissure is a variable branch of the pterygopalatine maxillary artery. It ascends from the pterygopalatine fossa towards the orbital apex and superior orbital fissure, where it can communicate with the anteromedial inferolateral trunk and ophthalmic circulation.

109. Superior pharyngeal tubal branch rightartery.amendment.superior_pharyngeal_tubal_branch.rightRelevance and clarity

Long account of a separate carotid branch distracts from the tubal branch.

Previous description

The superior pharyngeal branch gives off an Eustachian tube branch, which supplies the Eustachian tube and submucosa of the fossa of Rosenmüller.

The superior pharyngeal branch of the pharyngeal trunk contributes to the Eustachian tube anastomotic circle. It is connected to the petrous ICA’s mandibular and vidian arteries and forms connections with the accessory meningeal artery and pterygovaginal artery (distal IMA). It also sends a branch through the foramen lacerum to the cavernous sinus, joining the recurrent artery of the foramen lacerum and the ILT from the cavernous ICA.

The tubal branch of the superior pharyngeal artery supplies the pharyngeal opening of the auditory tube and adjacent nasopharyngeal mucosa. It communicates with accessory meningeal, pterygovaginal, vidian and mandibular arterial contributions around the tube.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

The artery of the falx cerebelli ascends within the small dural fold between the cerebellar hemispheres. It arises variably from posterior meningeal, ascending pharyngeal, vertebral or PICA-related dural branches.

137. ICA petrous rightartery.anterior.internal_carotid_right.segment.petrousAnatomical correction / qualification

Incorrect stellate-ganglion attribution and imprecise segment start.

Previous description

From the carotid canal to the petrolingual ligament (approximately the anterior aspect of the petrous ridge).

Course: the segment starts vertically in the carotid canal before turning anteromedially and running over the cartilage covering the foramen lacerum. It is accompanied by sympathetic fibres from the stellate ganglion and a venous plexus.

In the NYU classification, the petrous ICA extends from entry into the carotid canal to the petrolingual ligament. Its vertical and horizontal portions pass through the petrous temporal bone and over the cartilage of the foramen lacerum. It is accompanied by sympathetic fibres derived from the superior cervical ganglion and by a venous plexus.

Anatomy of the sympathetic pathways in the carotid canal

139. ICA paraophthalmic rightartery.anterior.internal_carotid_right.segment.paraophthalmicAnatomical correction / qualification

Replaces a mesh-estimated starting point with the stated NYU anatomical definition and removes an absolute imaging claim.

Previous description

From the estimated distal border of the cavernous segment, just proximal to the ophthalmic artery ostium, to the ostium of the PCOM.

This segment incorporates the proximal and distal dural rings (the clinoid ICA segment); however, the dural anatomy in the anterior clinoid region is complex, variable, and impossible to reliably define on imaging, making it difficult to differentiate intradural and extradural aneurysms in this region.

The NYU classification includes the clinoid and dural-ring transition within the paraophthalmic segment. The regional ring boundary is estimated here; the atlas does not resolve a separate dural-ring surface.

In the NYU classification, the paraophthalmic ICA extends from the estimated proximal dural ring to the posterior communicating artery origin. It includes the clinoid and ophthalmic regions and therefore spans the transition from extradural to intradural ICA. The ophthalmic origin alone does not reliably identify the distal dural ring.

Shapiro et al. Toward an Endovascular Internal Carotid Artery Classification System

153. Anterior choroidal rightartery.anterior.anterior_choroidal_rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Very broad territory list and an incorrect parenthetical placement of the red nucleus and subthalamus within the cerebral peduncle.

Previous description

The anterior choroidal artery supplies highly eloquent tissue. It is rarely absent. It usually arises posterolaterally from the ICA, 2-4 mm distal to the origin of the PCOM. It may uncommonly arise from the MCA, a joint origin with the PCOM, or the PCOM itself. It may also be larger than the PCOM, and thus occasionally mistaken for this vessel.

The anterior choroidal artery runs posteriorly beneath the optic tract and then around the cerebral peduncle in the carotid, crural, and ambient cisterns before again crossing the optic tract from medial to lateral at the level of the lateral geniculate body. It then enters the choroidal fissure and terminates in the choroid plexus of the lateral ventricle. Occasionally, it may sweep around the pulvinar to reach the foramen of Monro. It anastomoses with the posterior choroidal artery via its intraventricular terminal branches and also with the middle and posterior cerebral arteries.

The crucial branches are cisternal. They supply the optic tract, cerebral peduncle (corticospinal tracts, red nucleus, subthalamus), the posterior limb of the internal capsule, the optic radiation, the lateral geniculate body, proximal optic radiations, the hippocampus, the amygdala, the uncus, dentate gyrus, piriform cortex, globus pallidus, and the ventral thalamic nuclei. Although a small artery, its occlusion may cause significant neurological deficit, including contralateral hemiplegia, contralateral hemisensory loss, contralateral homonymous hemianopia, hemineglect, and aphasia.

The anterior choroidal artery usually arises from the supraclinoid ICA distal to the posterior communicating artery. It follows the optic tract and medial temporal region before entering the choroidal fissure to supply the lateral ventricular choroid plexus. Cisternal branches contribute variably to the optic tract, posterior internal capsule, pallidal, peduncular and medial temporal territories; these are not identical in every individual.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

155. Medial posterior choroidal rightartery.posterior.medial_posterior_choroidal_rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Incorrect description of the velum interpositum as a small membrane; excessive subsidiary territory detail.

Previous description

Medial posterior choroidal artery: this vessel usually arises from the medial P2 segment but may arise from the parieto-occipital, calcarine, or posterior pericallosal (splenial) branches. It runs medially and supplies the adjacent tectum, pineal gland, habenula and medial geniculate body, and posterior thalamus. It then enters the roof of the third ventricle within the velum interpositum (the small membrane just above and anterior to the pineal gland) to supply the choroid plexus. As it arches over the quadrigeminal plate, it adopts a characteristic ‘3’ shape on a lateral angiographic projection. It runs inferior to the lateral posterior choroidal arteries. Terminal branches anastomose with the lateral posterior choroidal arteries at the foramen of Monro.

The medial posterior choroidal artery usually arises from the proximal PCA, often P2, and curves around the midbrain towards the roof of the third ventricle. It enters the velum interpositum region to supply choroid plexus and gives variable branches to adjacent pineal, tectal and thalamic territories.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

163. ICA cervical rightartery.anterior.internal_carotid_right.segment.cervicalRelevance and clarity

Detailed nerve-position assertions distract from the segment and include an unnecessary accessory-nerve claim.

Previous description

This begins at the bifurcation of the CCA and ends as the artery enters the carotid canal.

Course: the ICA is contained within the carotid sheath, anteromedial to the IJV. The ECA is also usually anteromedial initially, before it veers laterally. The vagus nerve and the cranial root of the accessory nerve are located posteriorly.

The cervical ICA extends from the common carotid bifurcation to the external opening of the carotid canal. It ascends in the carotid space with the internal jugular vein and vagus nerve and normally gives no branches in the neck.

167. Lateral lenticulostriate 2 rightartery.anterior.lateral_lenticulostriate_2_rightRelevance and clarity

Unresolved phrase "this segment" and mixed medial/lateral perforator territories.

Previous description

Lenticulostriate arteries (between 1 and 20, average 10) arise from the superior surface of this segment and perforate the anterior perforated substance. The medial group supplies the globus pallidus and lentiform nucleus. The lateral group supplies the anterior commissure, internal capsule, and upper head and body of the caudate nucleus, putamen, globus pallidus, and substantia innominata. They have an S-shape configuration on the right, and reverse S-shape on the left. The medial group may also arise from the A1 and the latter group may arise from the M2 segments.

A representative lateral lenticulostriate artery arises from the MCA, most often its M1 segment, and enters the anterior perforated substance. These perforators supply variable portions of the striatum, pallidum and internal capsule. The numbered branches are illustrative and do not imply a fixed number or identical territories.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

Paramedian branches enter the pons off-centre.

A representative paramedian pontine artery arises from the basilar artery and penetrates the ventral pons near the midline. These perforators supply medial pontine territories; the numbered examples do not specify fixed universal branches.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy
179. Terminal ICA perforator 2 rightartery.amendment.terminal_ica_perforator_2.rightRelevance and clarity

Unresolved "this segment" and disease discussion replace the selected perforator anatomy.

Previous description

This segment contains a variable number of perforating arteries to the anterior perforating substance, which contribute to the collateral reconstitution in Moyamoya disease. Anastomoses are found with the posterior choroidal arteries and ACA.

A representative perforator from the terminal internal carotid artery enters the anterior perforated substance and contributes to deep forebrain supply. The number and territories of these perforators vary. They may enlarge in collateral networks in occlusive disease.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

181. VA medullary perforator 2 rightartery.amendment.va_medullary_perforator_2.rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Conflates a variable group of vertebral perforators with a single pontomedullary entry route.

Previous description

Perforators supplying the medulla and pyramids traverse the foramen caecum on the anterior medulla at the pontomedullary junction and penetrates all the way back to the tegmentum and floor of the fourth ventricle.

A representative direct medullary perforator arises from the intradural vertebral artery and enters the medulla. It contributes to medullary territories with variable overlap with anterior spinal and PICA perforators; there is no single obligatory entry through the foramen caecum.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

183. Precentral rightartery.anterior.precentral_rightRelevance and clarity

Repeated M4/MCA overview replaces this branch's own course or territory.

Previous description

The precentral artery is one of the frontal branches of the MCA. The cortical branches are named after the regions they supply.

The M4 (cortical) segments commence at the opercular turn and run on the surface of the cerebral convexity. The M4 cortical branches supply the cerebral convexity. The MCA as a whole also supplies the insular and opercular cortex through earlier branches, associated with the M2 insular and M3 opercular portions.

The precentral supplies the precentral and adjacent premotor cortex, often following the precentral sulcus. It is part of the MCA cortical arterial system; its origin and territorial overlap with neighbouring branches vary.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

The posterior parietal distal ramus supplies the posterior lateral parietal cortex. It is part of the MCA cortical arterial system; its origin and territorial overlap with neighbouring branches vary.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

Repeated M4/MCA overview replaces this branch's own course or territory.

Previous description

The M4 (cortical) segments commence at the opercular turn and run on the surface of the cerebral convexity. The M4 cortical branches supply the cerebral convexity. The MCA as a whole also supplies the insular and opercular cortex through earlier branches, associated with the M2 insular and M3 opercular portions.

The angular artery is named after the region it supplies.

The angular distal ramus supplies the angular gyrus and adjacent inferior parietal cortex. It is part of the MCA cortical arterial system; its origin and territorial overlap with neighbouring branches vary.

229. Angular–dorsal nasal rightartery.connection.angular_dorsal_nasal.rightRelevance and clarity

Whole-parent or network narrative does not isolate this named anastomosis; make its endpoints and potential nature explicit.

Previous description

Anastomoses to the distal ophthalmic artery include the distal facial artery via the dorsal nasal artery.

Angular artery: if present, this is the terminal branch of the facial artery. It may also originate from the ophthalmic or infraorbital arteries. The vessel courses in the nasojugal fold giving off the medial palpebral artery supplying the eyelids, and anastomoses with the lateral palpebral artery (lacrimal artery) to form the palpebral arcade. There are anastomoses with the ophthalmic, lateral nasal, and infraorbital arteries in addition to its counterpart across the midline.

This regional collateral pathway links Angular artery right and Dorsal nasal right. It represents a potential arterial communication; its calibre, visibility and recruitment vary.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

241. Occipital–vertebral C1 rightartery.connection.occipital_vertebral_c1.rightRelevance and clarity

Whole-parent or network narrative does not isolate this named anastomosis; make its endpoints and potential nature explicit.

Previous description

The occipital artery, derived from embryological type I and II proatlantal arteries (C1 and C2 segmental arteries), maintains anastomotic pathways from the ECA to the vertebral artery via posterior radicular branches at the C1 and C2 levels. These connections serve as major collateral pathways from the vertebral artery to the carotid system, particularly visible in cases where the common carotid is ligated or occluded.

This regional collateral pathway links Occipital artery right and VA muscular C1 right. It represents a potential arterial communication; its calibre, visibility and recruitment vary.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

283. Occipital mastoid–APA sigmoid rightartery.connection.occipital_mastoid_apa_sigmoid.rightRelevance and clarity

Whole-parent or network narrative does not isolate this named anastomosis; make its endpoints and potential nature explicit.

Previous description

A large mastoid branch often continues superiorly and then enters the cranium through the mastoid foramen to supply the posterior fossa dura mater: the posterior meningeal artery. It divides into descending, ascending, and posteromedial branches. The descending branch courses along the sigmoid sinus and anastomoses with the sigmoid sinus branch of the ascending pharyngeal. The ascending branch runs posterosuperiorly along the sigmoid sinus before anastomosing with the petrosquamous branch of the MMA and sometimes the subarcuate branch of the AICA. The posteromedial branch courses inferomedially and anastomoses with the hypoglossal, jugular, and musculospinal branches of the ascending pharyngeal, or the jugular branch of the posterior meningeal artery from the vertebral artery.

This posterior fossa dural collateral pathway links Occipital mastoid dural branch right and Jugular sigmoid branch right. It represents a potential arterial communication; its calibre, visibility and recruitment vary.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

Whole-parent or network narrative does not isolate this named anastomosis; make its endpoints and potential nature explicit.

Previous description

Paramedian arteries: these are the terminal branches of the MMA. They are directed anteriorly or posteriorly along the superior sagittal sinus and anastomose with the anterior (from the anterior ethmoidal artery) or posterior (from the ascending pharyngeal) falcine arteries. They deliver small branches to the superior sagittal sinus and falx cerebri. They also anastomose with dural branches of the posterior cerebral artery (PCA; Davidoff and Schechter) and the superior cerebellar artery (SCA; artery of Wollschlaeger and Wollschlaeger).

This falx cerebri collateral pathway links Anterior falcine right and MMA paramedian right. It represents a potential arterial communication; its calibre, visibility and recruitment vary.

290. Posterior choroidal–splenial rightartery.connection.posterior_choroidal_splenial.rightRelevance and clarity

Whole-parent or network narrative does not isolate this named anastomosis; make its endpoints and potential nature explicit.

Previous description

Posterior pericallosal (or splenial) branches: these small vessels originate from the P3 segment, parieto-occipital, calcarine, or posterior temporal arteries. They can form a common trunk with the medial posterior choroidal artery or can anastomose with the latter via a recurrent branch to the fornix. They are directed posteriorly initially and then turn abruptly anterosuperiorly to reach the corpus callosum. They anastomose with the terminal branches of the pericallosal artery.

This choroidal collateral pathway links Medial posterior choroidal plexal representative right and PCA splenial right. It represents a potential arterial communication; its calibre, visibility and recruitment vary.

331. Occipital mastoid–APA sigmoid leftartery.connection.occipital_mastoid_apa_sigmoid.leftRelevance and clarity

Whole-parent or network narrative does not isolate this named anastomosis; make its endpoints and potential nature explicit.

Previous description

A large mastoid branch often continues superiorly and then enters the cranium through the mastoid foramen to supply the posterior fossa dura mater: the posterior meningeal artery. It divides into descending, ascending, and posteromedial branches. The descending branch courses along the sigmoid sinus and anastomoses with the sigmoid sinus branch of the ascending pharyngeal. The ascending branch runs posterosuperiorly along the sigmoid sinus before anastomosing with the petrosquamous branch of the MMA and sometimes the subarcuate branch of the AICA. The posteromedial branch courses inferomedially and anastomoses with the hypoglossal, jugular, and musculospinal branches of the ascending pharyngeal, or the jugular branch of the posterior meningeal artery from the vertebral artery.

This posterior fossa dural collateral pathway links Occipital mastoid dural branch left and Jugular sigmoid branch left. It represents a potential arterial communication; its calibre, visibility and recruitment vary.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

335. Anterior falcine–MMA paramedian leftartery.connection.anterior_falcine_mma_paramedian.leftRelevance and clarity

Whole-parent or network narrative does not isolate this named anastomosis; make its endpoints and potential nature explicit.

Previous description

Paramedian arteries: these are the terminal branches of the MMA. They are directed anteriorly or posteriorly along the superior sagittal sinus and anastomose with the anterior (from the anterior ethmoidal artery) or posterior (from the ascending pharyngeal) falcine arteries. They deliver small branches to the superior sagittal sinus and falx cerebri. They also anastomose with dural branches of the posterior cerebral artery (PCA; Davidoff and Schechter) and the superior cerebellar artery (SCA; artery of Wollschlaeger and Wollschlaeger).

This falx cerebri collateral pathway links Anterior falcine left and MMA paramedian left. It represents a potential arterial communication; its calibre, visibility and recruitment vary.

338. Posterior choroidal–splenial leftartery.connection.posterior_choroidal_splenial.leftRelevance and clarity

Whole-parent or network narrative does not isolate this named anastomosis; make its endpoints and potential nature explicit.

Previous description

Posterior pericallosal (or splenial) branches: these small vessels originate from the P3 segment, parieto-occipital, calcarine, or posterior temporal arteries. They can form a common trunk with the medial posterior choroidal artery or can anastomose with the latter via a recurrent branch to the fornix. They are directed posteriorly initially and then turn abruptly anterosuperiorly to reach the corpus callosum. They anastomose with the terminal branches of the pericallosal artery.

This choroidal collateral pathway links Medial posterior choroidal plexal representative left and PCA splenial left. It represents a potential arterial communication; its calibre, visibility and recruitment vary.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

The lateral clival artery supplies the dura of the clivus. It has lateral and inferolateral branches which follow, respectively, the superior and inferior petrosal sinuses. There are anastomoses with its contralateral counterpart, the jugular branch of the ascending pharyngeal, and the MMA.

This clival collateral pathway links Lateral clival MHT branch right and Lateral clival MHT branch left. It represents a potential arterial communication; its calibre, visibility and recruitment vary.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

The vein of Trolard is a variable superior anastomotic superficial cerebral vein connecting the superficial Sylvian venous system with the superior sagittal sinus. It drains adjacent frontoparietal cortex and may be small, duplicated or absent.

Frontal cortical veins drain the frontal surface through a variable superficial venous network. Superior and medial channels commonly reach the superior sagittal sinus, while inferior frontal channels may join superficial Sylvian or basal drainage.

372. External jugular vein rightvein.external_jugular.rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Incorrectly includes the superficial temporal vein as a separate obligatory contributor at the EJV origin.

Previous description

The EJV drains the deep face, scalp, and posterolateral neck. It originates from the convergence of the retromandibular, posterior auricular, and superficial temporal veins in front of the angle of the mandible. The vein runs caudally on top of the sternocleidomastoid to join the subclavian vein. Like the IJV, it has a valve just prior to its termination.

The external jugular vein usually forms from the posterior division of the retromandibular vein and the posterior auricular vein. It descends superficially across sternocleidomastoid and usually drains into the subclavian vein. Its formation and tributaries vary.

Abnormal Formation of External Jugular Vein and its Repercussions, cadaveric study

374. Pterygoid venous plexus rightvein.pterygoid_plexus.rightRelevance and clarity

Restricts a three-dimensional plexus to a single interval between temporalis and lateral pterygoid.

Previous description

The pterygoid plexus is a large venous network located between the temporalis and lateral pterygoid muscles. It drains into the maxillary vein and connects with the cavernous sinus via emissary veins. The plexus receives numerous tributaries, including the sphenopalatine, middle meningeal, deep temporal, pterygoid, masseteric, buccinator, alveolar, and palatine veins. It also receives blood from the inferior ophthalmic vein, deep facial vein, and infraorbital vein.

The pterygoid venous plexus occupies the infratemporal region around the pterygoid muscles, with superficial and deep components. It drains predominantly through the maxillary vein and communicates with the facial vein, orbital veins and cavernous sinus through variable channels.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

376. Facial vein rightvein.facial.rightRelevance and clarity

Generic assertion that veins mirror arteries obscures the facial vein's own course and drainage.

Previous description

The extracranial drainage of the head and neck mirrors the arteries. The supratrochlear and supraorbital veins drain to the facial vein, which is a tributary of the internal jugular vein. The deep facial vein connects with the pterygoid plexus.

The facial vein begins near the medial canthus as the continuation of angular venous drainage and descends across the face. It commonly joins the anterior retromandibular division to form the common facial vein draining to the internal jugular vein. It communicates with the ophthalmic veins and pterygoid plexus; venous patterns vary.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

The superior ophthalmic vein begins in the anterior superomedial orbit and runs posteriorly towards the superior orbital fissure to drain into the cavernous sinus. It communicates with facial and other orbital veins. This atlas includes an inferior ophthalmic tributary joining it in the posterior orbit.

401. Superior sagittal sinusvein.superior_sagittalAnatomical correction / qualification

Overly fixed claim that Trolard is always the largest superficial tributary.

Previous description

The superior sagittal sinus extends from the foramen caecum to the confluence of sinuses. It is located within the superior attachment of the falx to the calvarium. It receives the superficial cerebral veins from the medial and lateral cerebral hemispheres, the largest of which is the vein of Trolard.

The superior sagittal sinus runs in the attached superior margin of the falx cerebri towards the confluence of sinuses. It receives superficial cerebral bridging veins and meningeal and diploic tributaries. The vein of Trolard may provide a major lateral hemispheric connection, but individual venous dominance varies.

402. Vein of Galenvein.galenAnatomical correction / qualification

Clarify the selected deep collector and avoid an overly fixed termination landmark.

Previous description

This short subarachnoid vessel is formed by the midline convergence of the internal cerebral veins, below the splenium, beneath which it curves. It ends at the tentorial hiatus apex, where it joins the inferior sagittal sinus to become the straight sinus. The vessel also receives blood from the posterior pericallosal veins, the internal occipital veins, and veins of the posterior fossa.

The great cerebral vein of Galen is a short deep venous trunk beneath the splenium of the corpus callosum. It receives the internal cerebral veins and variable basal and posterior fossa tributaries, then joins the inferior sagittal venous system at the anterior end of the straight sinus.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

Previous description

These sinuses run inferiorly and laterally to connect the posterior aspect of the cavernous sinus to the jugular vein in the pars nervosa. They also receive blood from the internal auditory veins and infratentorial structures.

The inferior petrosal sinus runs along the petro-occipital junction from the cavernous sinus towards the jugular bulb or upper internal jugular vein. Its jugular termination and connections with clival and infratentorial veins vary.

405. Basal vein of Rosenthal rightvein.basal.rightRelevance and clarity

Fixed formation and termination wording underrepresents recognised basal venous variation.

Previous description

The basal vein of Rosenthal originates under the anterior perforated substance in the chiasmatic cistern medial to the uncus of the temporal lobe, runs posteriorly, and terminates in either the vein of Galen or internal cerebral vein. It is formed by the confluence of the anterior cerebral vein and deep middle cerebral vein. Its second segment encircles the cerebral peduncle, and its third segment is located behind the midbrain. It connects to the superior petrosal sinus via the lateral mesencephalic vein.

The basal vein of Rosenthal is a deep venous collector beginning near the anterior perforated substance, commonly receiving anterior cerebral, deep middle cerebral and inferior striate drainage. It courses around the midbrain and usually reaches the Galenic system. Its segments may be incomplete and alternative petrosal, tentorial or other drainage routes occur.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

It is formed by the confluence of the anterior cerebral vein and deep middle cerebral vein. Its second segment encircles the cerebral peduncle, and its third segment is located behind the midbrain. It connects to the superior petrosal sinus via the lateral mesencephalic vein.

The basal vein of Rosenthal is a deep venous collector beginning near the anterior perforated substance, commonly receiving anterior cerebral, deep middle cerebral and inferior striate drainage. It courses around the midbrain and usually reaches the Galenic system. Its segments may be incomplete and alternative petrosal, tentorial or other drainage routes occur.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy
407. Superior petrosal vein rightvein.superior_petrosal_vein.rightAnatomical correction / qualification

Absolute largest-vein claim and a broad list of unrelated venous groups.

Previous description

The superior petrosal vein, or petrosal vein, is the largest vein in the posterior cranial fossa. It drains the anterior cerebellum and brainstem into the superior petrosal sinus. It is usually formed by multiple smaller veins converging into one, but sometimes there are two or three. Major contributors include the vein of the cerebellopontine fissure, and the petrosal, posterior mesencephalic, anterior pontomesencephalic, and tentorial groups.

The superior petrosal vein is a major collector of the petrosal cerebellar surface and adjacent brainstem. One or several trunks enter the superior petrosal sinus, receiving variable cerebellopontine fissure, peduncular and pontine tributaries.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

622. Foramen ovale rightlandmark.ovale.rightLandmark focus / anatomy

Description centres on an artery passing through the landmark instead of the opening, connections and contents.

The foramen ovale opens between the middle cranial fossa and infratemporal region in the greater sphenoid wing. It transmits the mandibular nerve (V3), emissary venous channels and variable small vessels including an accessory meningeal contribution.

626. Foramen lacerum rightlandmark.foramen-lacerum.rightLandmark focus / anatomy

Description centres on an artery passing through the landmark instead of the opening, connections and contents.

Previous description

The petrosal ICA runs over the cartilage covering the foramen lacerum.

The vidian artery from the IMA runs through the vidian canal to the foramen lacerum, where it meets the vidian branch of the petrous ICA.

The foramen lacerum lies at the junction of the sphenoid, petrous temporal and basilar occipital regions and is largely filled by cartilage in life. The internal carotid artery passes over its superior aspect rather than through an open vertical lumen. Petrosal neural and small vascular connections relate to this region.

Incorrectly equates vertebral passage through the atlanto-occipital membrane with intradural entry.

Previous description

The descending palatine artery enters the greater palatine canal accompanied by the great palatine nerve. Within the canal it gives off the lesser palatine artery, which supplies the soft palate.

The greater palatine canal descends from the pterygopalatine fossa towards the palate, between the maxillary and palatine regions. It carries the descending palatine vessels and palatine nerves.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy
653. Greater palatine foramen rightlandmark.greater-palatine-foramen.rightLandmark focus / anatomy

Description centres on an artery passing through the landmark instead of the opening, connections and contents.

Previous description

The descending palatine artery becomes the greater palatine artery as it exits the canal through the greater palatine foramen. It courses anteriorly beneath the hard palate to supply the hard palate, gingiva, and nasal septum.

The greater palatine foramen opens on the posterolateral hard palate. It transmits the greater palatine neurovascular bundle, which then runs anteriorly along the palate.

655. Mandibular canal rightlandmark.mandibular-canal.rightLandmark focus / anatomy

Description centres on an artery passing through the landmark instead of the opening, connections and contents.

Previous description

The inferior alveolar artery enters the mandibular canal through an opening on the medial surface of the mandibular ramus and then courses anteroinferiorly and medially towards the mental foramen on the anterior surface of the mandible.

The mandibular canal runs within the mandible from the mandibular foramen on the medial ramus towards the mental and incisive pathways. It contains the inferior alveolar nerve and vessels.

657. Atlas vertebral artery groove rightlandmark.atlas-groove.rightLandmark focus / anatomy

Description centres on an artery passing through the landmark instead of the opening, connections and contents.

Previous description

The vertebral artery imprints a groove on the posterior arch of the atlas, which may rarely form a bony ring called the arcuate foramen (a possible risk factor for paediatric ischaemic stroke).

The vertebral artery groove lies on the superior surface of the posterior arch of the atlas. The extradural vertebral artery crosses it before turning towards its dural entry.

659. Arcuate foramen (when present) rightlandmark.arcuate-foramen.rightLandmark focus / anatomy

Description centres on an artery passing through the landmark instead of the opening, connections and contents.

Previous description

The vertebral artery imprints a groove on the posterior arch of the atlas, which may rarely form a bony ring called the arcuate foramen.

An arcuate foramen is a variable bony bridge over the vertebral artery groove of the atlas. When present, it partly or completely roofs the regional vertebral neurovascular corridor.

661. Petrolingual boundary (estimated) rightlandmark.ica.petrolingual.rightLandmark focus / anatomy

Description centres on an artery passing through the landmark instead of the opening, connections and contents.

Previous description

From the carotid canal to the petrolingual ligament (approximately the anterior aspect of the petrous ridge).

From the petrolingual ligament to the proximal dural ring.

The petrolingual ligament forms a regional boundary between the petrous and cavernous ICA in the NYU classification. It extends near the petrous apex and sphenoid lingula; its position is estimated in this atlas.

Shapiro et al. Toward an Endovascular Internal Carotid Artery Classification System

663. Anterior clinoid process rightlandmark.ica.anterior-clinoid.rightLandmark focus / anatomy

Description centres on an artery passing through the landmark instead of the opening, connections and contents.

Previous description

The anterior clinoid process is superolateral to the genu of the ICA. Dural coverage extends medially to the tuberculum sellae from the anterior clinoid process. The dural ring is denser and more tightly adherent superolaterally and slopes inferomedially across the ICA so the medial and posterior aspects of the ICA genu can lie in the subarachnoid space.

The anterior clinoid process is the medial projection of the lesser sphenoid wing beside the optic canal and clinoid ICA. It relates to the optic strut, proximal and distal dural rings and the carotid-oculomotor region.

Existing atlas reference material and retained catalogue anatomy

665. Proximal and distal dural ring region rightlandmark.ica.dural-rings.rightLandmark focus / anatomy

Description centres on an artery passing through the landmark instead of the opening, connections and contents.

Previous description

This segment incorporates the proximal and distal dural rings (the clinoid ICA segment); however, the dural anatomy in the anterior clinoid region is complex, variable, and impossible to reliably define on imaging, making it difficult to differentiate intradural and extradural aneurysms in this region.

The proximal and distal dural rings bound the short clinoid ICA region. The distal ring marks the usual transition to the intradural ICA, with a variable carotid cave medially. This atlas marks a regional estimate rather than separately segmented dural rings.

Shapiro et al. Toward an Endovascular Internal Carotid Artery Classification System
